Volume 104 / Issue 199 / October 16, 2026

News

Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: ICE Brent highest since May as shipping attacks threaten Middle East supply
UKMTO reports 12 attacks on shipping in past week
Middle East crude exports slow in October
Black Sea war risk premiums hit 4-year high
Front-month ICE Brent settled at the highest level since May on Oct. 16 as traders as stepped-up attacks on shipping near the Strait of Hormuz pressured Middle East supply.
ICE December Brent climbed $3.94/barrel to $109.86/b and was the highest since May 19, while NYMEX November WTI settled $3.44/b higher at $101.41/b, the highest since Sept. 17.
"The Middle East conflict continues to dominate supply conditions," SAXO Head of Commodity Strategy Ole Hansen said in an Oct. 16 note. "Attacks on vessels in and around the Strait of Hormuz, combined with exceptionally high freight and insurance costs, continue to disrupt the economics of moving crude and refined products."
Twelve ships have been attacked in or near the Strait of Hormuz in the past seven days, according to a UK Maritime Trade Operations report released Oct. 16.
Middle East crude exports are trending lower in October, even as traffic through the Strait of Hormuz recovered to 16 transits on Oct. 15 from 12 the previous day, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Total Middle East crude exports, including Saudi volumes from Yanbu, are tracking at 11.9 million barrels/day so far in October, down from 15 million b/d in September, preliminary CAS data showed.
(continued on page 26)

International Crude
Azeri Light differential drops by record $5.33/b on MOC activity
Highest daily change in Azeri Light differential on record
Premiums fall over $7/b in two days
Platts assessed Azeri Light CIF Augusta $5.33/b lower day on day at a $10.36/b premium to Dated Brent on Oct. 15, the largest daily move on record, after three trades were done in the Platts Market on Close assessment process on the day.
A day after the Platts MOC assessment process ended with three outstanding offers Oct. 14, sellers returned with their cargoes, starting with BP offering a Nov. 10-14 delivery at a $15.70/b premium to Dated Brent. Itochu soon joined, offering a Nov. 4-8 cargo at the same level.
By the time Socar joined the offer side at around 1530 London time, BP and Itochu had lowered their offers to below $13/b, a drop of around $3/b from when they were first shown at around 1430.
Socar's offer was lifted by OMV at an $11.45/b premium, while BP and Itochu had their offers lifted at $10.20/b and $10.40/b, respectively, shortly before the MOC close.
Socar subsequently reoffered a cargo, which remained outstanding at the end of the pricing session at a $10.55/b premium to Dated Brent.
"It's like trying to catch a falling knife," one Mediterranean trader said of the rapid decline in differentials.
Offer levels fell in increments of 5 cents/b per minute, with BP and Itochu making more than 100 price moves over two hours before tradeable levels were found.
At a $10.36/b premium to Dated Brent, Azeri Light returned to levels last seen around Sept. 14, when Platts assessed the grade at plus $9.80/b.
The correction in Azeri Light differentials came as other regional grades, including Es Sider and Saharan Blend, continued to soften.
"High prices that we saw last week eroded refining margins, and the market turned long with all the supply available," a second Mediterranean source said.
The trajectory of Azeri Light differentials, however, differed from that of North African grades, which are assessed on an FOB basis and did not receive the same support from elevated freight rates as delivered Azeri Light.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 16, 2026

Es Sider and Saharan Blend rose in late September before gradually tapering off, while Azeri Light continued to strengthen into October.
Azeri Light subsequently fell by more than $7/b over Oct. 13-15 as elevated freight costs, weaker refining margins and the start of the November trading cycle contributed to a correction in differentials.
-- Jan Kedzior
China unlikely to refill oil reserves without sharp price drop
Backwardation deters strategic buying plans
Imports rise as depleted commercial stocks cut additional supplies
December arrivals expected to drop on costs
China is unlikely to replenish its crude stocks unless oil prices and freight costs fall sharply, two crude procurement managers and a crude trader at Chinese state-run companies, as well as an executive at a state think tank, told Platts Oct. 16.
Improving imports over September-November would slow China's destocking activities, as logistics uncertainties have led to uneven crude arrivals, while inflows are likely to fall in December due to high prices, the sources added.
"The market is in backwardation, and we cannot rule out the possibility of a sudden drop in crude prices, making it an unfavorable time for stockpiling," the crude trader said.
On Oct. 16, the Singapore-assessed physical Dubai time spread was at $2.69/b, indicating backwardation despite narrowing from $3.695/b the day before, Platts data showed.
"We still largely take a hand-to-mouth approach amid the volatile market, but logistics uncertainties mean the pace of deliveries can fluctuate," said a crude procurement manager at a state-run refinery, adding that the company has had to increase crude imports only because its depleted commercial crude stocks can no longer provide additional supplies.
Another crude procurement manager said state-run refineries can now pay market prices for crude barrels drawn from commercial storage, rather than physically returning them within a specified timeframe, as was required previously.

China's crude imports reached a five-month high of 9.06 million barrels/day (37.1 million mt) in September, according to data from the General Administration of Customs.
It was also the first time China's crude imports crossed the 9-million-b/d mark since the 9.4 million b/d seen in April amid the ongoing conflict in the Middle East, although the volume was 21.5% below 11.55 million b/d in September 2025, GAC data showed.
The inflows are set to continue improving in October and November, as deliveries from Saudi Arabia increase, Platts reported earlier.
However, the first crude procurement manager said the volume is likely to slow down in December due to the high costs.
The freight for a 270,000 mt VLCC carrying crude from the Arab Gulf to China remained at a high level of $243.24/mt Oct. 15, despite falling from the record high of $255.72/mt Oct. 12, according to Platts data.
China's total crude stocks fell to 1.252 billion barrels in endSeptember from 1.307 billion barrels in end-August and the record high of 1.382 billion barrels in end-May, according to satellite data from Ursa Space. The volume fell further to 1.242 billion barrels as of Oct. 15, Ursa Space data showed.
-- Staff
Middle East crude exports fall in H1 Oct amid Hormuz security concerns: CAS
Exports drop to 11.9 mil b/d in Oct
Hormuz transits rebound to 16 ships Oct. 15
Iran reports tanker fire
Middle East crude exports are trending lower in October, even as traffic through the Strait of Hormuz recovered to 16 transits on Oct. 15 from 12 the previous day, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Total Middle East crude exports, including Saudi volumes from Yanbu, are tracking at 11.9 million barrels/day so far in October, down from 15 million b/d in September, preliminary CAS data showed.
The decline may reflect fewer tanker transits through the Strait of Hormuz amid heightened security concerns following a recent wave of attacks on merchant vessels, though CAS cautioned that increased dark activity could be obscuring actual flows, particularly ship-to-ship transfers in the Gulf of Oman.
On Oct. 16, Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps said a tanker was on fire in the Sea of Oman after attempting to transit through what it called a "restricted area," according to a statement carried by state broadcaster IRIB.
Strait of Hormuz traffic on Oct. 15 comprised six vessels observed departing the Gulf and 10 moving inbound, the CAS report said. Five transits were Iran-linked, involving a USsanctioned product tanker, a US-sanctioned LPG carrier and three landing craft, the report said.
At the US blockade line just beyond the Strait of Hormuz,

CAS tracked 71 vessel transits, comprising 42 inbound and 29 outbound. Only two were non-compliant: one inbound and one outbound bulk carrier, the report said.
At the Bab al-Mandab Strait, the region's other major maritime chokepoint, 41 vessels transited the waterway, with 22 moving northbound into the Red Sea and 19 southbound toward the Gulf of Aden, according to CAS.
Despite a "maritime embargo" on Saudi Arabia declared by Yemen-based Houthi militants, two Saudi-linked ships crossed the Bab al-Mandab on Oct. 15, including a bulk carrier and a livestock carrier that had previously called at Saudi ports, the CAS report said.
-- Lauren Holtmeier, Aresu Eqbali
Russia's first cargoes of Vostok oil set to arrive in China by Oct. 19
Winter conditions to complicate supplies from Nov
Most deliveries shipped via Arctic Northern Sea Route
Total Russian exports via NSR exceed 8 mil barrels this year
The first two cargoes of Russian Vostok oil are due to arrive in China by Oct. 19, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, as Russia bids to develop a major new oil supply network from Northern Russia.
Russia's largest oil producer Rosneft, which is developing Vostok Oil, officially launched the project on Sept. 6, when it loaded the first oil at the Bukhta Sever Port on the Kara Sea.

Most supplies are traveling via the Northern Sea Route, which runs through Russia's Arctic waters with a navigational season typically starting from June-July, and provides significant time and cost savings compared to alternatives.
CAS data shows two tankers -- the Vladimir Monomakh and the Vladimir Vinogradov -- both loaded with 100,050 mt of crude and condensate approaching China. Another tanker, the Ravelin, carrying Vostok Oil is traveling via the Atlantic and is due to arrive in China by the end of October.
Analysts, however, see winter weather conditions as a significant challenge to export plans.
Independent energy analyst George Voloshin said that without ice-breaker escorts, open-water and standard ice-class tankers must wrap up operations by late October, as the open-water navigation window in the Yenisey Gulf and Kara Sea closes rapidly between late October and mid-November.
"Given the current global shortage of Arc7 crude carriers dedicated to Vostok Oil, regular commercial liftings will taper off sharply once heavy ice forms, unless Russia diverts high-ice-class tonnage from other Arctic basins," he added.
Russia exported 8.3 million barrels of crude, condensate and fuel oil via the NSR to China or East Asian waters for offshore transfers in June-September, including 3.9 million barrels of Urals crude from Primorsk, according to CAS. This compares with 9.91 million barrels for the whole of 2025. The estimates do not take into account ships carrying cargo from the Russian Arctic and western ports that are sailing eastwards but have not yet passed the Bering Strait.

Crude oil futures volume traded on India's Multi Commodity Exchange, or MCX, gained 9.27% month over month to 1.610 million lots in September, exchange data showed Oct. 16, amid increased Middle East crude flows to India.
The MCX crude oil futures contract is a cash-settled contract, priced and traded in Indian rupees, and was first listed on Oct. 11, 2005. One lot is equivalent to 100 barrels.
The MCX crude oil futures contract is settled based on the NYMEX WTI crude oil prices and incorporates crudes of mostly light, sweet quality, with sulfur content of below 0.42% and API gravity that ranged between 37 and 42 degrees.
The total cumulative open interest across the futures contracts on Sept. 30 stood at 15,024 lots, up 21.55% from the open interest on Aug. 31, where the contracts closed the month at 12,360 lots.
Meanwhile, Indian imports of Russia's Urals reached an alltime high of 63.2 million barrels in July but have since fallen to 46 million barrels, down 27%, even as total crude flows into India rose by 3.9 million barrels to 163.3 million barrels between July and September, Kpler data showed.
The redirection of some of the Urals flows from India to China coincided with an increase in flows from the Middle East to India, which reached 82.7 million barrels in September -- near the 86.9 million barrels seen in February before the US-Israel war with Iran began and much above the 51.5 million barrels reported in August.

Itochu sells its first crude oil cargo in Platts Mediterranean MOC
Itochu sells Azeri Light on its second MOC appearance
Trading activity picks up after release of Nov loading program

Itochu sold a cargo of Azeri Light ex-Ceyhan CFR Augusta in the Oct. 15 Platts Market on Close assessment process, the first time the entity has sold a crude oil cargo in the Mediterranean MOC, Platts data showed.
At 4:25 pm London time, five minutes before the end of the MOC, ENI lifted Itochu Trading for a cargo of Azeri Light CFR Augusta arriving Nov. 4-8. The cargo was purchased at a $10.40/b premium to Dated Brent.
Platts assesses Azeri Light CIF Augusta, but CFR indications are also allowed, subject to normalization.
Itochu sold its cargo on its second day of participating in

the MOC after it offered barrels for the first time Oct. 14. On both days, the Japanese entity was joined in the assessment process by BP and SOCAR, which also offered cargoes of Azeri Light.
Azeri prices reached an all-time high of $18/b premium to Dated Brent Oct. 8 as expensive freight and elevated diesel cracks supported the differentials of the diesel-rich grade. But with time, high crude prices compressed refinery margins, prompting a correction in prices of Mediterranean sweet grades.
"Freight is coming off, and the rise in Azeri prices was partly due to the freight," a Mediterranean source said. "The refining margins also thin, and so I am not surprised by the fall in the differential."
Another factor that facilitated price discovery for the grade was the release of the November loading program toward the end of the week that ended Oct. 9. This allowed traders to abandon their wait-and-see approach, which is uncommon between trading cycles, and demonstrate value through trading of November-loading barrels.
-- Jan Kedzior

Iraq hands Kirkuk oil fields over to BP, targeting 450,000 b/d crude output
Four fields have been handed over
Output targets 500 MMscf/d of gas
ConocoPhillips holds 42% project stake
Iraq has transferred operations of its Kirkuk oil fields to BP with plans in motion to boost crude production to 450,000 barrels/day and gas output to 500 million standard cubic feet/ day, Iraq's oil ministry said in a statement Oct. 15.
Four oil fields -- Kirkuk, Bai Hassan, Khabbaz and Jambur -- previously operated by Iraq's state-owned companies will now be operated by BP, the statement said.
BP did not respond to a request for comment Oct. 15. In February 2025, BP and Iraq reached a production-sharing deal to redevelop the Kirkuk fields and establish a new operator comprising personnel from BP and Iraq's state-owned North Oil Company and North Gas Company. BP agreed in July to sell a 42% interest in the Kirkuk oil development project to ConocoPhillips. The Kirkuk fields produce about 320,000 b/d, with some volumes feeding domestic refineries and others exported via Turkey's Ceyhan port. The agreement between BP and Iraq for the Kirkuk fields includes production of more than 3 billion barrels of oil equivalent from the Baba and Avanah domes of the Kirkuk oil field and three adjacent fields -- Bai Hassan, Jambur and Khabbaz. There is also potential for investment in exploration under the deal, with the area possibly holding 20 billion boe, BP said previously. Production at the fields has long been in decline, following a peak of about 1 million b/d in the decades after the reservoir's discovery in 1927. The original Kirkuk discovery is central to BP's early heritage. Despite ample remaining reserves, the Kirkuk fields needed significant rehabilitation following the Iraq War in 2003. BP undertook technical studies at Kirkuk intermittently between 2013 and 2019, in parallel with its role at the Rumaila field in the south of the country.
-- Lauren Holtmeier
AUSTRALIA DATA: Aug crude, condensate exports jump 44% on month as crude output rebounds
Malaysia becomes top disclosed export market
Crude oil production surges 39% in August
Inventories rise despite lower import volumes
Australia exported 8.33 million barrels of crude oil and other refinery feedstocks in August, jumping 44.4% month on month but 12.4% below year-earlier levels, the latest Australian Petroleum Statistics data from the Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water showed.

Exports in the month averaged 268,710 barrels per day, rising from a revised 186,129 b/d (5.77 million barrels) in July, and compared with 306,774 b/d (9.51 million barrels) in August 2025.
Cargoes recorded under "No Country Details" remained the largest component, rising 31.5% month on month to 6.91 million barrels, accounting for around 83% of total August exports.
Among disclosed destinations, Malaysia was the largest recipient at 953,534 barrels, sharply higher from a low base of 2,516 barrels in July. Singapore received 269,832 barrels, followed by Japan at 88,057 barrels and Taiwan at 70,446 barrels.
The August data comes amid active trading in Australian condensate and light sweet crude for October loading, with South Korean refiners featuring prominently among buyers.
Woodside Energy was heard to have sold its Pluto and North West Shelf condensate cargoes for October loading to South Korea's Hanwha, while Japan's Mitsubishi was heard to have moved a North West Shelf condensate cargo loading Oct. 20-24 to a South Korean refiner.
INPEX was heard to have sold two Ichthys Field condensate cargoes loading Sept. 30-Oct. 4 and Oct. 28-Nov. 1 to Hanwha at premiums equivalent to the $5s-$7s/b and the $8s/b, respectively, against Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, FOB.
INPEX was also heard to have sold another Ichthys condensate cargo loading Oct. 12-16 to ExxonMobil at premiums in the $7s/b against the same benchmark.
In the light sweet crude market, Santos was heard to have sold an Oct. 18-22 loading Cooper crude cargo to Vitol at premiums in the $20s/b to Platts Dated Brent, FOB.
Crude output rebounds Australia produced 6.71 million barrels (216,452 b/d) of crude
oil and condensate in August, up 3.6% month on month but 6.9% below August 2025 levels.
The increase was driven by crude oil production, which jumped 39.2% from July to 1.09 million barrels, although this was 42% lower year on year.
Condensate production edged down 1.3% month on month to 5.62 million barrels, but was 5.6% higher year on year.
Condensate continued to dominate Australia's liquids production, accounting for about 84% of combined crude and condensate output in August.
LPG output slipped 0.7% month on month and 4.6% year on year to 2.73 million barrels.
Crude imports fall as inventories build Australia imported 4 million barrels (129,032 b/d) of crude oil
and other refinery feedstocks in August, down 19.7% from July and 13.2% from a year earlier.
Total oil imports across all products fell 8.2% month on month to 29.88 million barrels (963,871 b/d), and were 8.4% below August 2025 levels.
Despite lower imports, crude oil and refinery feedstock inventories increased 4.6% month on month to 9.37 million barrels at end-August, which was also 4.5% above year-

SINGAPORE DATA: Crude oil imports fall 2.1% week over week to 673,892 mt
Middle East accounts for 89% of crude oil imports First Papua New Guinea import since May

Singapore's crude oil imports inched down 2.1% week over week to 673,892 metric tons Oct. 8-14, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 15.
The Middle East accounted for 89% of the total crude oil imports, with 135,920 mt from Oman and 461,132 mt from the UAE, the data showed.
Imports were also recorded from Brazil at 5,500 mt and Papua New Guinea at 71,340 mt. This marks the first significant crude oil import from Papua New Guinea, whose sole export grade is light sweet Kutubu Blend crude, since the week to May 27, according to the data.
A negligible 0.004 mt of condensate was imported from Australia in the latest week, the sole condensate shipment for the week.
Singapore's crude oil imports averaged 97,307 metric tons/ day Oct. 1-14, down from 99,070 mt/d in September, according to Enterprise Singapore data compiled by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
The city-state's condensate imports averaged 3,186 mt/d Oct. 1-14, down from 9,824 mt/d in September, the data showed.
-- Yong ren Toh

CRUDE MOC: Platts Dubai differential falls below $5/b premium
Platts cash Dubai, Oman at $4.83/b premium
Two Dubai partials trade during MOC

Cash differentials for Middle East medium sour crude markers declined for the sixth day during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 16 amid thin trade.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai and cash Oman both at a premium of $4.83/barrel to same-month Dubai futures at the market close, down $1.56/b day over day.
December cash Murban was down 30 cents/b over the same period at a premium of $7.40/b to the same-month Dubai futures.
During the MOC, two December Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded, with Glencore and Mitsui selling one partial each to Trafigura.
No convergences were reached during the MOC. A convergence occurs when 20 partials are traded between two counterparties in the same direction, resulting in a full 500,000-barrel physical cargo being declared from the seller to the buyer.
Cash Brent values were on the cusp of flipping back into a premium against cash Dubai, the first time it would be doing so since mid-August. The front-month December cash Brent-Dubai spread was assessed by Platts at minus 83 cents/b Oct. 16, up $2.165/b on the day and the highest since Aug. 26 when it was at minus 72 cents/b.

Peru shifts away from refined products imports in Oct, prioritizing crude: CAS
No refined product shipments scheduled so far in Oct
Crude blend purchases rise to 4.49 million barrels
Talara flexicoker outage forces lighter crude: CERA
Peru's imports of crude and refined products have shifted its focus to crude grades so far in October, with shipments of refined products falling nearly 41% on the month, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea collected on Oct. 16.
Total import volumes of refined products and crude so far in October are set to drop 40.3% month over month to 4.49 million barrels in October from 7.52 million barrels in September. The

number of shipments also looks set to decline to 17 from 31, the CAS data shows.
Specifically, Peru's refined product import volumes are on track for a significant month-on-month decline in October, with total arrivals dropping by nearly 41% compared with September, according to CAS.
Instead, Peru has shifted to importing crude blends, with Brazilian and Guyana Blends accounting for the majority of volumes at 2.12 million barrels and 2.09 million barrels, respectively.

Peruvian imports of crude by blend (million barrels)

Blend

Oct-26

Brazilian Blend

2.12

2.09

Canadian Blend

2.10

Netherlands Blend

0.70

Total

4.49

Source: CAS

Comparatively, September's imports were dominated by refined products such as diesel, gasoline and fuel oil.
The shift comes amid reports of ongoing operational issues at state-run Petroperu's Talara refinery, according to S&P Global Energy CERA analyst João Lopes.
"When Talara has to take a unit like the flexicoker offline, Pertroperu is forced to reduce utilization and cut production of refined products," said Lopes. "That unit normally converts fuel oil into lighter products, so if the conversion capacity is constrained, Peru has to change the crude slate, buying lighter crude grades so the refinery does not end up with excess fuel oil."
Peru has been impacted by high crude and refined products prices amid the war in the Middle East, with expectations for further increases, Lopes said.
"As barrels become scarcer, the country could struggle to secure suppliers for middle distillates," he added.
Despite an increase in domestic demand, Lopes said the country appears to have enough supply.
"The country managed to navigate the recent [diesel price increase] crisis without drawing down stocks too aggressively, but that buffer cannot last indefinitely if imports do not keep pace," he said. "The concern is less about today's balance and more about what lies ahead."
-- Jaime Martínez

European E5 gasoline premium over E10 widens on tight oxygenates

Blenders shift to reformate amid shortages
European E5 Eurobob gasoline oxy blends continue to trade at considerable premiums to E10 non-oxy blends amid tight

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 16, 2026

oxygenate supply and elevated blending costs. Platts pricing data showed the FOB AR E5 Eurobob barge
premium over E10 widening $2.25/mt to $48/mt on Oct. 15, having consistently traded above $20/mt since the beginning of October.
MTBE, ETBE premiums surge amid tight supply Tight MTBE availability has supported prices for oxygenated
gasoline blends across European markets amid muted arrivals from Asia in recent weeks. Around 160,000 mt of MTBE was expected to arrive in Europe over October, according to Kpler data as of Oct. 16.
Platts assessed the FOB ARA MTBE premium over the M1 E5 swap at $676.50/mt on Oct. 15, unchanged day over day but still its highest since Oct. 12, 2023.
ETBE premiums have strengthened even further. Platts assessed the FOB AR ETBE premium over the E5 swap at $976.50/mt on Oct. 15, up $526.50/mt year over year. ETBE is produced using ethanol as a feedstock and is particularly used as a gasoline blendstock in Mediterranean markets including Spain and Italy.
Against this backdrop, market participants said high MTBE prices had discouraged its inclusion in gasoline blends, with blenders increasingly looking to alternative octane components.
"MTBE [is] no longer in [gasoline] blends," a European broker said, referring to reduced blending demand amid the product's ongoing supply shortage.
"MTBE [is] quite short," a European trader said. Ethanol, another high-octane component also used in the ETBE supply chain, has meanwhile held comparatively firm against gasoline despite recent declines in outright values. Platts assessed T2 FOB Rotterdam ethanol barges at $780/mt on Oct. 15. According to Platts pricing data, the gasoline-ethanol spread, measuring the difference between FOB AR E5 Eurobob barges and FOB Rotterdam T2 ethanol barges, narrowed $33.50/mt to $411.50/mt from the beginning of October through Oct. 15.

Insights Global data. Stocks remained well below the 562,000 mt recorded on Oct. 16, 2025.
At the same time, market participants have reported firm petrochemical demand, with rising propane prices supporting naphtha buying for steam cracking as the relative economics of the two feedstocks narrowed.
"Naphtha demand is good, prices are going up on cracking [demand]," a naphtha market participant said. "[It's] probably due to LPG prices."
Despite the stock build, gasoline blenders have also reported difficulties sourcing product.
"Naphtha seems to be hard to find," a gasoline trading source said.
Naphtha also serves as feedstock for refinery reformers producing reformate, allowing demand from the petrochemical and gasoline blending sectors to overlap. However, market participants have expressed mixed views over the extent to which reformate production is contributing to tight physical naphtha availability.
-- Friedrich Gutsche
Brazil gasoline imports to reach 273,789 cubic meters by Oct. 20
Netherlands supplies 53% of October imports
Russia sends 127,893 cubic meters
Import arbitrage window closes for 233 days
Brazil is expected to receive at least 273,789 cubic meters of gasoline imports through Oct. 20, according to preliminary data from port lineups, sources, and S&P Global Commodities at Sea on Oct. 16.
At least six ships are scheduled and confirmed to discharge imported gasoline at Brazilian ports in October. Most will already have arrived, berthed, or discharged.

Reformate substitution shifts focus to naphtha Tight oxygenate availability has prompted market participants
to look to alternative octane boosters such as reformate, with supply able to keep pace with demand from blenders.

"Reformate seems quite supplied now, actually," a European trader said.
The substitution has emerged alongside a sharp contraction in gasoline blending economics against naphtha.
Platts assessed the front-month gasoline-naphtha spread at $180.50/mt on Oct. 15, down $6.75/mt day over day. The spread has narrowed by $94.25/mt since the beginning of October.

Gasoline stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp hub fell 1.55% to 826,000 mt in the week ended Oct. 15, a seven-week low amid various factors accelerating destocking in the region.
Trading activity for Eurobob barges had been observed to be strong amid strong backwardation structures supporting incentives to sell stored quantities into the market.
As a result of the market drawing on inventories, Platts assessed the FOB AR Eurobob barges down $9/mt to $1,190.25/ mt on Oct. 15, adding to a significant $130/mt downward move since the beginning of the week.

Naphtha ARA naphtha stocks rose 71,000 metric tons, or 27.84%,
week over week to 326,000 mt, up from 255,000 mt on Oct. 8, according to Insights Global data.
Despite the week-over-week increase, total stocks remain well below the level from this time last year, when they stood at 562,000 mt on Oct. 16, 2025.
Over the last week, rising propane prices may have prompted an uptick in naphtha buying by the petrochemical sector as propane-naphtha spreads narrow, sources said.
"Naphtha demand is good, prices are going up on cracking [demand]," a naphtha market participant said. "[It's] probably due to LPG prices."

West Coast gasoline imports hit five-year high after refineries went dark: CAS
India emerges as third-largest fuel supplier in 2026
Import sources expand from 11 to 15 nations
Mexico receives 65% of regional fuel exports
Nearly a year after Phillips 66 permanently shut its 139,000 b/d Wilmington, California refinery, the US West Coast fuel market has undergone a structural shift in its import sourcing and export patterns, with gasoline imports reaching their highest level in at least five years and the supplier base expanding to 15 countries

as the region scrambles to replace lost domestic refining capacity.
The closure of Wilmington in October 2025, followed by Valero's accelerated shutdown of its 145,000 b/d Benicia, California refinery in February 2026 -- two months ahead of its originally scheduled April 2026 closure date -- removed a combined 284,000 b/d of California refining capacity within a matter of months, triggering a sustained and evolving response in seaborne trade flows into PADD 5.
Imports surge, exports constrict Total refined product imports to the US West Coast reached
about 61.1 million barrels between January and October 2026, with jet fuel dominating at roughly 24.7 million barrels, or 40% of the total. Gasoline was the second-largest product at about 14.1 million barrels, followed by CARBOB at 8.45 million barrels and alkylate at 5.28 million barrels. Monthly import volumes peaked in March at 9.70 million barrels and April at 8.95 million barrels, before easing to around 6.1 million barrels in July and August. On the export side, USWC shipments from January through September totaled 32.65 million barrels, with ULSD and diesel accounting for about 67% of that volume.
Gasoline flows shift The most significant structural change has been in gasoline
imports, which reached 19.07 million barrels in 2026 through the available reporting period -- a 54.5% increase from 12.34 million barrels in 2025 and the highest level seen over the last five complete years, with the fourth quarter still to run.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

12

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 16, 2026

South Korea remained the leading single source, supplying 4.59 million barrels in 2026, though slightly below the 4.92 million barrels recorded in 2025. Canada held its position as the second-largest origin at 3.88 million barrels, down from a surge of 4.50 million barrels in 2025. The most striking development was the emergence of India as a major supplier. Indian gasoline volumes rose to 3.83 million barrels in 2026, around 4.5 times the 852,152 barrels recorded in 2025, placing India just behind Canada and close to South Korea in the supplier rankings. The shift reflects India's growing refining export capacity and its ability to competitively serve arbitrage windows into California.
Europe also returned as a meaningful supplier after a quieter 2023-2025 period. The Netherlands, Belgium, Norway, and the UK together supplied about 1.46 million barrels in 2026, compared with around 441,000 barrels from identified European origins in 2025. The Netherlands alone contributed 637,676 barrels. European supply had previously reached roughly 1.29 million barrels in 2022, making 2026 a resurgence rather than an entirely new development.

Canada, and Japan. The supplier base broadened substantially, with imports
arriving from 15 countries in 2026, compared with 11 countries in each year from 2023 through 2025. The "Others" category -- which included Trinidad and Tobago, the US Virgin Islands, Senegal, and Norway -- grew from 845,381 barrels in 2025 to 2.30 million barrels in 2026.
Increased reliance on finished gasoline Following Phillips 66's March 2025 announcement that
Wilmington would close in October 2025, CARBOB imports rose sharply to 1.65 million barrels in October 2025 and reached a then-period high of 1.98 million barrels in November, consistent with the market beginning to replace Los Angelesarea refining capacity with seaborne supply. Finished gasoline imports also rose, climbing to 1.67 million barrels in November and 1.54 million barrels in December, from 1.03 million barrels in October.

In contrast, gasoline export flows from the US West Coast contracted sharply and became highly concentrated within the last ten months. Reported gasoline shipments in 2026 went only to Mexico and Guatemala, totaling around 3.10 million barrels, with Mexico accounting for roughly 65% of that volume at around 2 million barrels. This marks a significant narrowing from the broader destination mix seen in 2023, when exports reached 11 countries, including Malaysia, the UK, Honduras, South Korea,

The import response intensified around the Benicia closure. After Valero moved the shutdown forward to February 2026, gasoline imports climbed from 1.13 million barrels in February to 2.14 million barrels in March and 2.45 million barrels in April, while CARBOB rose from 1.28 million barrels in February to 2.63 million barrels in March and 1.79 million barrels in April. March and April 2026 produced the strongest combined gasoline and CARBOB inflows in the available series, though seasonal demand patterns, inventory positioning, refinery maintenance cycles, and arbitrage economics may also have contributed to the timing.
The composition of replacement supply shifted as the year progressed. While CARBOB dominated the initial response to the Wilmington and Benicia closures, finished gasoline imports remained elevated even as CARBOB volumes fell sharply in later months. August 2026 gasoline imports reached 2.22 million barrels while CARBOB imports were zero, indicating a greater reliance on finished gasoline, domestic blending components, or other blendstocks during that period. By October 2026, the market was operating with a higher structural baseline of gasoline imports, though without a consistent one-for-one relationship with CARBOB arrivals.

Jet
Switzerland allows temporary release of jet fuel stocks
Zurich Airport faces fuel shortage risk
30,000 cubic meters to be released through Nov. 1
Switzerland has temporarily authorized the release of jet fuel stocks to maintain supplies at Zurich Airport, the Swiss Federal Office for National Economic Supply, or FONES, said Oct. 15.
The release has been attributed to cancellations and delays in rail freight and low Rhine water levels, which could mean that the airport is at risk of running out of fuel, FONES said in a statement.
Up to 30,000 cubic meters can be drawn between Oct. 15 and Nov. 1, FONES said. The volume corresponds to 6.5% of the available obligatory jet fuel stocks.
The measure is being applied because of rail delays exacerbated by low Rhine water levels, which have redirected the shipment of goods from river to rail, further burdening the rail network, according to the statement.
Without the stock release, Zurich Airport would not have sufficient stocks to cover the missing deliveries, FONES said.
FONES noted that while the supply of gasoline, diesel and heating oil has deteriorated compared with the previous week, it "remains secure."
In late September, Switzerland allowed the release of some stocks due to an outage at the Cressier refinery.
-- Elza Turner
Japan to subsidize difference between domestic kerosene wholesale and import prices: minister
New kerosene subsidy system to be in place from Nov utnil March 2027
Government addresses winter supply concerns
Refiners urged to maintain normal sales levels
Japan is set to introduce a new system to subsidize the difference between domestic kerosene wholesale and import prices amid high import prices of the middle distillate, Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry Ryosei Akazawa said Oct. 16.
Amid high kerosene import prices, "We will therefore introduce a system under which the difference between import prices and domestic wholesale prices will be subsidized through an imported kerosene adjustment rate," Akazawa told reporters in Tokyo.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene outright price at an average of $175.60/b to date in October, compared with September's average of $169.50/b and the 2025 average of $86.42/b.
The minister's comments came as METI is aware that some wholesalers and other businesses are placing orders substantially above the usual level of demand, and that some refiners and

distributors have been asking their customers to place orders in line with expected demand to maintain stable supplies.
"We are aware that these developments have raised concerns among some members of the public about kerosene supplies during the winter. However, I want to reiterate that we are by no means in a situation where supplies are running short. I would first like to make that point clear," Akazawa said.
Amid kerosene supply concerns, Akazawa said, "We will ask refiners and distributors, import trading companies, wholesalers and other businesses to keep sales and orders at the same level as in the corresponding month of the previous year."
Aakazawa added, "We will establish a new direct kerosene sales scheme, under which oil refiners and distributors will sell kerosene directly in cases where supply becomes uneven, or bottlenecks develop in distribution."
"The subsidy scheme for the price difference will be effective from Nov. 2026 to Mar. 2027, though its details will be finalized later," Nobuhiko Kikkawa, Director of Fuel Distribution and Retail Office in the Agency for Natural Resources and Energy, part of METI, said at a press briefing in Tokyo. "The current price difference is a little less than ¥20/l."
According to Kikkawa, METI has set up a consultation platform for supply disruptions in oil products or lubricant markets on its website since March, which is also to be used for consumers or traders who struggle to procure kerosene.
"METI will help consumers or traders find refiners' spots that supply kerosene cargoes to those in need of kerosene as fast as possible, while consumers or traders have to talk with refiners on how much kerosene cargoes cost," Takehiko NAGAI, Director of Industrial Safety Policy Division in METI, said.
Asked about South Korea's imports, Kikkawa said there is no problem for Japan's refiners or importers to procure kerosene cargoes from South Korea as far as METI acknowledges.
South Korean supply Japan's major external kerosene supplier, South Korea, is
already preparing term and spot shipments for the NovemberMarch cold season, product marketing managers at two refiners based in Ulsan and Seosan told Platts on Oct. 16.
"Depending on Japan's domestic production and demand balance, South Korea typically exports 3 million to 5 million barrels of kerosene to Japan during the November-March period,"

ARA jet fuel stocks rise 3.13% week over week: Insights Global
Jet fuel inventories rise to 494,000 mt
Jet regrade remains positive on tight supply
IEA announces stock release
Jet fuel stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp refining hub rebounded 3.13% week over week to 494,000 metric tons as the jet-diesel regrade strengthened, reversing the previous week's draw but remaining critically low amid ongoing tightness in the European market, according to Oct. 15 data from Insights Global.
Despite the recent gains, inventories remain below the 543,000 mt recorded in mid-September, indicating that supply conditions remain relatively tight by recent standards.
Total oil inventories in the ARA hub rose marginally to 4.04 million mt from 4.01 million mt a week earlier. The increase was driven largely by higher jet fuel and fuel oil stocks, which offset declines in gasoline inventories.
The jet fuel crack spread against dated Brent was $90.87/ barrel Oct. 15, putting it at a $2.07 premium to ULSD, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy. The jet-diesel regrade fell $3.72 on the week but remained positive as market participants reported that jet fuel "cannot tighten further" and that more production is needed.
A source familiar with the matter reported that refineries are mostly maximizing jet production to take advantage of these favorable refining economics.
This supply build comes as the IEA announced Oct. 15 that members will release more than 20 million barrels of diesel as soon as possible and accelerate the rest of its emergency oil draw.
The outlook remains mixed. While the second consecutive build in jet fuel inventories may alleviate some immediate supply concerns, stock levels remain below September highs. Concerns over supply continue amid ongoing supply disruptions and renewed refinery attacks in Russia.
-- Oskar Leskow, Aruni Sunil
Europe jet/kerosene imports reach 1.6 million mt so far in October
UK, European jet fuel intake at 372,000 mt this month
Higher jet yields not expected to reach market until Nov

Europe's jet fuel and kerosene imports reached 1.6 million metric tons over Oct. 1-14, lower than the 2 million mt imported during the whole of September, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
In October so far, Europe's imports included 361,000 mt from India, higher than the 197,000 mt imported in September, and 545,000 mt from South Korea, so far lower than 571,000 mt discharged in September.
The US discharged 95,000 mt of jet and kerosene into Europe in October so far, sharply lower than 339,000 mt in the month of September, while Nigeria discharged 133,000 mt in October so far versus 386,000 mt in September.
The UK imported 435,000 mt of jet in Oct. 1-14, compared with 737,000 mt for the whole of September.
Spain has taken in 372,000 mt in October so far, higher than the 177,000 mt seen last month, while the Netherlands imported 393,000 mt, up from 380,000 mt in September.
European jet differentials softened Oct. 15 as the market remained tight, with the jet-diesel regrade remaining firmly in favor of jet production.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the jet CIF Northwest Europe cargo differential down $16.25/mt day over day to a $201.50/mt premium to front-month ICE low sulfur gasoil, with the jet FOB FARAG barge differential falling $25/mt to a $191.75/mt premium.
The market was tight, reflected in the strong differentials, market participants said, as refineries maximized diesel over jet over the last two months. This led to the regrade flipping, with the jet FOB barge physical crack assessed $7.49/b over the diesel barge crack, incentivizing refiners to produce more jet.
Diesel crunch However, while a wider regrade would typically signal yield
switching at refineries, traders said the market remained cautious.
"The diesel crunch at the moment makes it ... difficult to switch to jet fuel," a Europe-based jet trader said. "But there has been a stronger regrade for a few weeks. Refineries are taking time to plan a change."
The trader said higher jet fuel yields were not expected to reach the market until November.
"I think they will switch back over to jet fuel," a European diesel trader said. "That could [also] support US diesel cracks."
Observing jet fuel/kerosene imports into Europe so far this year, 4.2 million mt have been discharged from the US, 2.2 million mt from Nigeria, 1.8 million from India, 1.5 million mt from Kuwait, 1.5 million mt from Saudi Arabia and 1.1 million mt from the UAE.
Looking ahead, some 1.9 million mt of jet fuel is so far scheduled to arrive in Europe in October, lower than the 2.2 million mt imported in the same period last year, Kpler shipping data showed.

Diesel
Russian diesel exports fall, expected to rebound after partial ban lifted
Russian diesel exports fell early October
Partial export ban lifted Oct. 9-10
Exports expected to rebound after ban
Russian diesel and gasoil exports fell in the week to Oct. 8, but the partial lifting of the ban, announced Oct. 9, is expected to push flows higher in the coming weeks.
The Russian government partially lifted its diesel export ban Oct. 10, following negotiations with US administration officials.
The US Treasury Department's Office of Foreign Assets Control issued a sanctions waiver Oct. 9, allowing the sale, delivery, and import of Russian-origin diesel fuel, including imports into the US. The license is effective until April 7, 2027.
Before the ban was lifted, Russia exported 69,000 metric tons of diesel and gasoil in the week ended Oct. 8, down from 119,000 mt the previous week, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data. These were the second lowest weekly loadings on record, according to CAS data, which dates back to 2016.
Flows are expected to rebound from Oct. 9, with preliminary CAS data showing exports of 152,000 mt from Oct. 9 to 16, subject to revision.
Most loadings in the week to Oct. 8 came from Primorsk, with 30,000 mt loaded in Vladivostok.
Of the 69,000 mt of product in the week to Oct. 8, 39,000 mt were loaded for Turkey, with the reminder unknown. So far in October, from Oct. 1 to 12, 39,000 mt of product were loaded for Turkey, compared to a total of 60,000 mt in September, while 217,000 mt were loaded in August, according to CAS data.
Total loadings of Russian diesel and gasoil from Oct. 1 to 16 reached 260,000 mt, subject to revision, whereas the total in September was about 449,000 mt. For comparison, total Russian diesel and gasoil loadings were about 2.3 million mt in September 2025, while in October last year they reached nearly 2.7 million mt.
Refining capacity Almost half of Russia's primary refining capacity was offline
at the end of September due to Ukrainian attacks, according to S&P Global Energy CERA analysts. Ukrainian strikes on Russian refineries slowed in October amid mounting pressure on Ukraine from the US administration.
US President Donald Trump has continued to blame Ukrainian

attacks for high diesel prices, with the fresh comments made in an interview with Newsmax Oct.15. He expects Ukraine to stop striking diesel-producing Russian facilities. While criticizing Ukraine, Trump does not comment on Russia, which has also continued to attack Ukrainian energy infrastructure.
Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said Oct. 12 that Ukraine has received no commitment from Russia to stop striking its energy facilities. Zelenskyy reiterated that Russia must stop striking Ukraine's energy system and killing Ukrainians if it wants Ukraine to halt its strikes against Russian oil refineries and oil exports.
Commenting on the US-announced agreement on Russian diesel supply, Russian Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak said Russia was ready to supply 300,000 mt of diesel to the US market and other partners in October and 500,000 mt in November.
Novak later said that Russia could increase its diesel exports to global markets to as much as 3.5 million metric tons/month by the end of 2026, depending on the availability of refining infrastructure.
"Given that our diesel production capacity is double the domestic market's requirements, it is sufficient--even when accounting for maintenance work--to both meet domestic demand and sustain exports, in line with the figures we discussed," Novak said to Russian media in a televised interview Oct.16.
-- Alexandra Vladimirova, David Neef

India's exports of middle distillates rose to 876,000 metric tons in the week ended Oct. 11, up from the 628,000 mt shipped in the week prior, driven by an increase in diesel/gasoil exports, mostly loaded at the port of Sikka, S&P Global Commodities at Sea data showed.

The main recipients of Indian diesel/gasoil are expected to be Spain, taking 208,000 mt, up from 100,000 mt the previous week and Brazil, taking 140,000 mt. This is expected to be followed by Egypt, taking 104,000 mt, Namibia with 100,000 mt and South Africa, at 80,000 mt.

All is expected to come from PADD 3 (US Gulf Coast), with none expected to come from PADD 1 (Atlantic Coast).

are expected to reach Europe in October, compared with 391,000 mt that arrived in September, according to Kpler. All of these exports are expected to come from PADD 3. Based on historical data from Kpler, no jet fuel/kerosene arrived in Europe from the US in October 2025.

SINGAPORE DATA: Middle distillate stocks rise 10.72% to near 6-month high
Singapore flips to net importer of gasoil
Jet fuel exports rise nearly sevenfold
Singapore's onshore commercial middle distillate stocks rose 10.72% week over week to 10.38 million barrels over Oct. 8-14 amid an influx of gasoil imports, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 15.
Stocks were last higher in the week ended April 29 at 10.92 million barrels, marking a near six-month high, historical data showed.
The city-state flipped to being a net importer of gasoil over Oct. 8-14 as inflows rose fivefold to 255,694 mt over the period. Net imports were last seen over April 30-May 6, historical data showed.
China was the largest supplier with 102,512 mt, the most since Aug. 13-19 when the country shipped 136,753 mt of gasoil to Singapore, Enterprise Singapore data showed.
The remaining imports came from Malaysia with 64,799 mt, South Korea with 55,459 mt and India with 32,922 mt.
In contrast, exports slipped 29.85% week over week to 203,720 mt over Oct. 8-14, the data showed.

The Netherlands and Cambodia were the top destinations at 59,989 mt and 36,390 mt, respectively.
This was followed by Sri Lanka (32,480 mt), Malaysia (21,274 mt), and Togo (14,604 mt).
Exports to Indonesia fell to 12,576 mt from 54,583 mt the week before, while 10,010 mt flowed to Spain, according to the data.
The bulk of the remaining outflows was received by six other countries, with South Africa leading with 7,466 mt.
Jet fuel exports rise Singapore returned to being a net exporter of jet fuel/
kerosene over Oct. 8-14 as imports fell to 6,176 mt from 41,995 mt the week before, according to Enterprise Singapore data.
The downswing came amid a lack of arrivals from China, India, and Malaysia, which had bolstered imports in the week ended Oct. 8. In comparison, South Korea was the primary supplier in the week to Oct. 14, with 6,170 mt.
Bucking the decline, exports rose nearly sevenfold to 92,940 mt over Oct. 8-14, the data showed.
Australia and the Netherlands were the top destinations at 40,320 mt and 38,385 mt, respectively.
The remaining outflows were largely absorbed by five other countries, led by Reunion (8,994 mt) and Fiji (2,790 mt).

BUNKERWORLD INDEX: Bunkers climb amid strong freight, despite falling crude
Tight HSFO at Singapore narrows Hi-5
Rotterdam HSFO barge crack hits four-month high
Abundant USGC supply bucks the trend
The high costs of transporting 3.5% sulfur fuel oil have caused tightness at key bunker hubs, offsetting the effect of sliding physical crude oil benchmarks through the week ending Oct. 15.
The Platts Bunkerworld Marine Fuel 0.5% Sulfur Index ended the week at $890/mt, down $4/mt day over day, up $8/mt week on week and up $13/mt month on month. The Platts BW380 Index, which represents value for 3.5% sulfur fuel oil, ended the week at $792/mt, down $4/mt day over day, up $28/mt week over week and up $88/mt month over month.
The Platts Dated Brent crude oil benchmark moved lower through the week amid reports that Tehran and Washington have signaled a willingness to resume peace talks.
Also, International Energy Agency members will accelerate the release of emergency oil stocks, including more than 20 million barrels of diesel, as tight supplies continue to strain global fuel markets.
The IEA Governing Board said Oct. 15 that about 330 million barrels had been released under its March 2026 Collective Action, and members would take steps to complete the remaining releases of approximately 100 million barrels.

Singapore Hi-5 spread narrows to 8-month low as HSFO supply tightens
Hi-5 spread at $39.76/mt Oct. 15
HSFO supply constraints support prices

and 380 CST HSFO cargo -- also known as the Hi-5 spread -- to the tightest level in eight months.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Hi-5 spread at $39.76/metric ton Oct. 15, down $6.34/mt from the previous day. The spread, which has sharply come off from multiyear highs in August, was last lower at $34.94/mt Feb. 16, Platts data showed.
HSFO cargo availability has tightened in the region following the prolonged disruption to flows of heavy crudes and fuel oil out of the Strait of Hormuz while a slowdown in imports of heavy distillates from Russia further shrank the supply pool.
Costly tanker freight, together with a recent slew of vessel attacks at the Strait of Hormuz, also added to concerns that flows to Singapore would be constrained.
The lower sulfur fuel oil grade has been more bearish in comparison despite supply constraints, as the market braces for potentially more aggressive delivered bunker fuel prices at China's Port of Zhoushan.
China's larger-than-expected LSFO quotas have pressured prices, with the bunkering hub of Zhoushan less likely to be reliant on imports for fourth-quarter sales, market sources said.
A narrower Hi-5 spread typically incentivizes the blending of mid-sulfur components into the HSFO blend pool, which could help ease some of the supply tightness for HSFO.
Mirroring the fundamentals in the cargo market, the Hi-5 spread in the Asia-delivered bunker fuel market has also narrowed.
Downstream demand for the HSFO grade has likely taken a hit as a result, with three Singapore-based traders citing weaker HSFO bunker demand, now that LSFO appears cheaper in comparison, especially for prompt availabilities.
The spread between the Singapore-delivered 0.5%S marine fuel and 380 CST HSFO bunker fuel narrowed to a record low of $16/mt Oct. 15, down 40.7% day over day and 78.4% week over week, Platts data showed.
In north Asia, tightened HSFO supply has elevated premiums across the region. The Hong Kong week-to-date average Hi-5 spread narrowed to $8.25/mt from last week's average of $22.40/ mt, Platts data showed.
Given limited stocks of the fuel grade, multiple Hong Kongbased suppliers said during the week that HSFO quotations commanded a higher premium than the LSFO counterpart, subject to quantity and delivery date.
-- Deborah Lee, Iris Leung, Kahlil Sudhakaran

US DATA: Fuel oil stocks rise on decreased demand
Atlantic Coast inventories climb to 5.5M
Domestic production drops 35,000 b/d total
Consumption falls 39.9% during the week
US residual fuel oil stocks rose during the week ending Oct. 9, driven by decreased demand, despite lower import and production volumes observed during the period, according to Energy Information Administration data released Oct. 15.
US fuel oil stocks rose 286,000 barrels to 24.167 million, reaching their highest level since the week ended May 1, when stocks stood at 24.524 million barrels. The US Atlantic Coast accounted for the bulk of the increase, with levels rising 397,000 barrels to 5.5 million, while US Gulf Coast stocks decreased 316,000 barrels to 13.782 million during the same period.
Conversely, domestic production levels in the US fell, with total volumes declining by 35,000 barrels/day to 339,000 b/d. Production levels on the US West Coast fell 44,000 b/d to 93,000 b/d, while the USGC climbed 10,000 b/d to 166,000 b/d. Also rising, production volumes on the USAC rose 4,000 b/d to 31,000 during the same period.
US fuel oil imports also declined week over week, with total imports decreasing by 110,000 b/d to 161,000 b/d. The USAC experienced the largest decrease, with imports down 66,000 b/d to 62,000 b/d, while import volumes in the USGC decreased 32,000 b/d to 95,000 b/d.
Total US domestic demand -- as measured by total product supplied -- decreased week over week as well, with levels falling to an average of 184,000 b/d, down 122,000 b/d, or 39.9%.
Total product supplied "approximately represents consumption of petroleum products" by quantifying "the disappearance of these products from primary sources," according to the EIA.
In the physical USGC HSFO market, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Oct. 15 pricing up $3.68/b week over week to $86.78/b.
-- Humza Mirza
Feedstocks
Pakistan eases fuel oil export curbs as stockpiles build up
Pakistan energy regulator approves 195,000 mt fuel oil export
PARCO leads Pakistan fuel oil export quota at 60,000 mt

Pakistan's Oil and Gas Regulatory Authority has allowed four domestic refineries to export around 195,000 metric tons of fuel oil in October, according to a notification seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, amid rising stockpiles at the terminals.
Four refineries -- Pak Arab Refinery or PARCO, National Refinery, Pakistan Refinery and Cnergyico -- received permission from the regulator, according to the late September notification seen by Platts on Oct. 14.
The letter addressed to the refineries said "granted inprinciple approval for export of furnace oil subject to maintaining strategic reserves to meet the power sector requirement."
PARCO received the largest individual allocation of 60,000 mt, followed by Pakistan Refinery with 50,000 mt, Cnergyico with 45,000 mt, and National Refinery with 40,000 mt, according to the separately issued notification.
Earlier in March, Pakistan instructed domestic oil refineries to obtain prior government approval before exporting furnace oil, in an attempt to bolster reserves after the Iran war started disrupting Middle East oil flows, Platts reported earlier.
Pakistan's fuel oil exports have dropped in the first two months of the fiscal year 2026-27 (July-June) due to stronger demand from the country's power generation sector, as the war in the Middle East curbed LNG availability for utilities.
Oil refineries exported 153,122 mt of fuel oil in July-August, down 39% year over year, the latest data from the Oil Companies Advisory Council (OCAC) showed.
Domestic fuel oil consumption rose to 180,000 mt in the first two months of the FY 2026-27 from 30,000 mt a year ago, according to OCAC data.
The rise was driven by a more than threefold increase in electricity generation from fuel oil-fired power plants, which produced 321 gigawatt-hours in August alone, according to data from the National Electric Power Regulatory Authority published in the week of Sept. 21.
Fuel oil-fired power generation during July and August stood at 536 GWh, compared with 200 GWh in the preceding year, while electricity generation from re-gasified LNG plants slumped 36% year over year to 2,940 GWh during the same period, NEPRA data showed.
Despite the domestic demand surge, Pakistan's refineries remain commercially incentivized to export. "On a calorific value and considering the tax, it is more expensive to burn fuel oil than LNG [for power generation]," said a fuel oil trader based in Singapore.
Pakistan exported 1.435 million mt of high-sulfur fuel oil and 180,469 mt of low-sulfur fuel oil in FY 2025-26, up from 1.3 million mt of HSFO and 137,880 mt of LSFO in the previous financial year, according to OCAC data.
Platts assessed the 380 CST HSFO FOB Singapore cash differential over the Mean of Platts Singapore assessments at a premium of $49.58/mt on Oct. 15, up $1/mt from the previous day. The premium was at its highest level in a month, with the cash differential for the grade last higher on Sept. 15 at $53.67/mt.
-- Deborah Lee, Haris Zamir

PetroChina starts commercial operations at Tarim ethylene project
Adds 1.45 mil mt/year polymer, rubber capacity
PetroChina ethylene capacity reaches 12.03 mil mt/year
PP, MTBE output exported to Central Asia
PetroChina has started full commercial operations at Dushanzi Petrochemical's 1.2 million metric tons/year Phase II Tarim ethylene project in Xinjiang, adding new ethylene and downstream polymer supply in western China, the company said in Beijing Oct. 15.
The start-up lifts PetroChina's total ethylene capacity to 12.03 million mt/year and brings its ethylene capacity in Xinjiang to 3 million mt/year.
The project includes 900,000 mt/year of full-density polyethylene, 450,000 mt/year of polypropylene and 100,000 mt/ year of butadiene rubber capacity, taking its combined polymer and rubber capacity to 1.45 million mt/year.
Output ramps up The project has produced more than 260,000 mt of ethylene
and supplied 566,000 mt of chemical products since its July 16 start-up, with PP and methyl tertiary butyl ether already exported to Central Asian markets.
Eight major units, including full-density PE, PP and

butadiene rubber units, achieved on-spec production by July 25. The complex completed its first high-load performance assessment Sept. 24, with several operating indicators exceeding design levels.
The new capacity expands PetroChina's petrochemical production base in western China, while opening an export outlet into neighboring Central Asian markets, the company said.
The project is integrated with feedstock supply from the Tarim oil field, which has processed nearly 60 billion cubic meters of natural gas and produced more than 4 million mt of ethane to date.
-- Daisy Xu - Oil Market Specialist
Gas Liquids
NWE butane large cargo price rises to highest since November 2021
Butane large cargo price near 4 year highs
Refineries retain supply for gasoline blending
Import flows fail to ease regional shortage
Northwest European butane large cargoes have climbed to their highest levels since November 2021, with tightness spanning the entire regional butane complex, from large cargoes to coasters and barges.
The Platts CIF NWE butane large cargo price was assessed at 108.25% of naphtha, or $996.50/mt, on Oct. 15, which incidentally was the same percentage to naphtha as it had been on its previous high Nov. 16, 2021, as scarce prompt availability, refinery retention and rising seasonal gasoline blending demand continued to support values.
Market participants said coasters and barges were also extremely tight, highlighting that the squeeze was not limited to large cargoes but reflected a broader shortage of available C4 across Northwest Europe.
"There's literally nothing in NWE," one market source said, while another said they did "not see any C4 offers at the moment." A further market participant described the market as "insanely tight," saying there were "absolutely no sellers" aside from one visible seller.
The strength has been driven by refiners retaining more butane for internal gasoline blending requirements as wintergrade specifications allow higher C4 inclusion in gasoline pools. Supportive gasoline economics have reinforced the trend, keeping barrels inside refinery systems and reducing spot supply available to merchant buyers.
The adjacent West Mediterranean market was also described as extremely tight, with similar causes cited. Refiners in the region have also been holding back butane for winter gasoline blending, leaving little spot availability and keeping competition firm for prompt barrels.
Import relief has been limited, with transatlantic replenishment yet to provide a clear route to easing Northwest

Tankers
War risk premiums for Black Sea crude exports hit 4-year high amid ship attacks
Insurance costs surge to $4.6/barrel
Eight seafarers go missing in latest attack
Oil exports drop, tanker rates rise
War risk premiums for insuring crude shipments from Black Sea ports have reached their highest level in at least four years amid intensifying maritime attacks in the region.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the additional war risk premium at $4.60/barrel on Oct. 16, up from $4/b a day earlier, $3/b on Oct. 12, and the highest since the assessment began in October 2022.
The AWRP for crude exports from Black Sea ports, often carried on Aframaxes and Suezmaxes, had stabilized at roughly $3/b for several months after falling from a summer peak of $3.70/b on July 29, according to Platts assessments. This compares with 9 cents/b at year-end 2025.
Marcus Baker, Global Marine Head at the insurance brokerage Marsh, said the headline rate for ADWP is 5%-10% of a ship's hull value. "Black Sea rates ... have a big range, depending on vessel destination, cargo, flag, etc.," Baker told Platts Oct. 16.
The premium for a Suezmax transit through the Black Sea now ranges from $4 million to $5 million, a Geneva-based trader of Black Sea cargoes said.
Regional tanker rates have risen as oil exports have decreased amid worsening security conditions in recent months.
Crude and condensate exports from Russia's Black Sea ports, including Russian and Kazakh barrels, fell from 2.75 million b/ day in June to an average of 2.07 million b/d in the third quarter, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
The Suezmax rate for carrying 135,000 mt of Kazakh crude from the Black Sea to the Mediterranean is $135.14/mt Oct. 16, near its all-time high, compared with $33.08/mt June 30, according to Platts assessments.
Security consultancy Vanguard TECH reported that two tankers caught fire off Romania on Oct. 15, as the country's defense ministry reported drone attacks. Eight crew members aboard the ship Wine Trader went missing during the incident, according to Vanguard.

"The situation in the Black Sea is serious. Commercial ships are being attacked and sunk. Seafarers killed, injured, and missing, very close to the shores of Romania and Bulgaria," Romanian President Nicuor Dan said in an X post Oct. 15. "This is unacceptable and it has to stop."
Since early summer, Russia and Ukraine have launched more intense attacks on merchant ships involved in each other's trades, with consultancy Windward estimating 86 ships attacked during July-September, up from 23 in the first half of 2026.
In mid-September, Lloyd's insurers expanded war-risk areas -- where ADWP is charged -- in the region to cover the entire Black Sea except for territorial waters not belonging to Russia or Ukraine.
"Commercial shipping risk in the Black Sea now extends well beyond Ukrainian and Russian port approaches," consultancy Dryad Global said in a note earlier this month. "Attacks involving aerial drones and uncrewed surface vessels are widening the exposure facing merchant ships, with consequences for crew safety, voyage planning and trade continuity."
-- Max Lin, Jan Kedzior
WAF Suezmax rates hit 2026 highs, pulls VLCCs higher before market cools
Suezmax rates hit record w1000 in H1 October
VLCC freight climbs to 2026 high of w792.5 Oct. 9
Markets correct as costs turn unsustainable
The West Africa dirty tanker market witnessed high volatility in the first half of October, driven by a record-breaking rally in the Suezmax segment that briefly lifted the VLCC market before a sharp correction as freight costs reached unsustainable levels.
Suezmax market reaches unprecedented heights The Suezmax market entered October in a firm state, with a
tight tonnage list struggling to cover a steady flow of fresh cargo inquiries. The rally accelerated as cargoes piled up into the third decade and owners remained firmly in the driver's seat.
By the middle of the first week, the market had entered what participants described as a "new dimension," with the benchmark WAF-UKC route reaching an all-time high. The market's extreme tightness was underscored by reports that production could soon face a "no-show risk" due to an outright shortage of vessels.
However, these unprecedented rate levels proved unsustainable. The first signs of resistance emerged as fixtures began to fail, with refiners struggling to absorb the extreme freight costs. Charterers started actively exploring VLCCs as a more economical alternative for several Suezmax stems.
The market began a sharp correction in the second week, as a clear test at lower levels signaled a new trading range and sharply lower paper markets made charterers cautious. The market ended the period seeking stability after high volatility.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

23

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 16, 2026

VLCC market firms in tandem The VLCC market in West Africa began the month on a quiet
but firm note. While fresh cargo inquiries were limited, a tight list of vessels committed to the Atlantic meant that owners had the leverage to resist any downward pressure. The sharp uptick in the Suezmax segment was the primary catalyst for a firming of VLCC sentiment, as charterer economics began to swing back in favor of the larger vessel class.
The firming trend was confirmed by a market quote from Brazil, which drew a series of high offers, with rates reportedly rising significantly. Charterers facing a tough audience and limited options were forced to pay up, pushing the market to new 2026 highs.
However, similar to the Suezmax segment, the VLCC market began to cool in the second week. With activity in the East starting to slow and a Brazil fixture trading at a lower level, sentiment began to soften. The quiet days and sporadic demand took their toll and while owners were keen to lock in long voyages at the elevated levels, the upward momentum had clearly stalled.
The high volatility was reflected in freight assessments. The Platts assessment for the benchmark 130,000 metric tons WAF-UK/Continent Suezmax route surged from w745 on Oct. 1 to a 2026 high of w1,000 on Oct. 5, before correcting to w920 by Oct. 15. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. The VLCC market followed a similar trajectory, albeit less extreme. The Platts assessment for the West Africa-Far East 260,000 mt route climbed from w520 on Oct. 1 to a year-to-date peak of w792.5 by Oct. 9, before easing to w712.5 by the end of the period.
-- Cresida Rodrigues
News
Syria's oil, gas pipelines come under further attack
Gas pipeline explosion halts power supply
Sabotage targets Banias-Homs product line
Crude imports drop to zero since August
Syria's state energy company reported two separate pipeline attacks on Oct. 15, including an explosion on a key domestic gas transmission line, as the country's energy systems face a mounting wave of attacks while it works to attract investment and repair its war-torn infrastructure.
A 16-inch al-Jabsah gas pipeline was struck by an explosion in the al-Arabidi area in northeastern Syria, the Syrian Petroleum Company said in a statement. Emergency teams shut sectional valves at both ends of the damaged section -- at the al-Jabsah gas plant and the Deir ez-Zor gas station in the al-Bughailiyah area -- while firefighting and security teams were dispatched to the scene, the company said.
The al-Jabsah line connects to Syria's central gas network, supplying Homs and surrounding areas with power. As of January,

the pipeline was carrying approximately 1.2 million cubic meters/ day, SPC said earlier this year.
The Banias-Homs petroleum products pipeline was also struck in what SPC described as an act of sabotage carried out for the "purpose of theft" near Homs, resulting in a gasoline leak. Repair efforts are underway, SPC said. The 24-inch pipeline carries products inland from Syria's Banias port.
Escalating attacks The twin strikes on Oct. 15 are the fourth and fifth incidents
in a series of attacks that have targeted Syrian oil and gas infrastructure.
A Syrian oil pipeline in the al-Hasakah area was damaged in an attack on Oct. 7. In the nearby Deir ez-Zor province, a fire broke out on a gas pipeline in the Shula area on Sept. 29, knocking some power stations out of service, while on Oct. 1, an explosion at the Tishreen pressure reduction station damaged a pipeline, though pumping resumed the following day, according to SPC statements.
In mid-August, an explosion on the same al-Jabsah gas line halted gas flows and disrupted electricity generation, which SPC described at the time as a "deliberate act of sabotage."
Syria is working to rebuild its oil and gas sector following a prolonged civil war, but the country continues to face security threats, including from remaining pockets of Islamic State fighters, though the militant group's activity has fallen to record low levels, according to a September analysis by the Middle East Institute. Local regional media reported that suspects have been arrested in connection with the latest attack, though neither the perpetrators nor a motive have been formally identified.
The attacks have coincided with widespread diesel shortages and a mid-September price hike that helped spur protests over the cost of living. Syria's energy ministry said at the time that the price increases were temporary and reflected higher procurement costs and the country's dependence on imported supplies.
Since the ouster of former President Bashar al-Assad at the end of 2024, Syria has relied primarily on Russia for crude imports and scattered donations from other nations, including Saudi Arabia. However, S&P Global Commodities At Sea data showed that Syria imported between 35,000 and 90,000 barrels/day of crude per month throughout most of 2026, but no imports were recorded in August, September or so far in October.
Syria's imports of gasoline and diesel reached 43,000 b/d in September, up sharply from around 25,000 b/d throughout much of 2026, CAS data showed. Syria operates two refineries that process crude into products primarily for the domestic market, but domestic crude production, while climbing over the past year, remains below the levels needed to meet internal demand.
Syria is working to import greater volumes of fuel to meet local demand, with SPC announcing Oct. 15 that a shipment of about 30,000 metric tons of diesel had arrived at Banias.
-- Lauren Holtmeier
