INDIA DATA: India exports 1 mil b/d refined oil products, says Oil Ministry
Refining capacity to expand 8.6% by 2027
India aims at 6.4 mil b/d refining capacity by 2032
India exports around 1 million barrels/day of refined oil products into the global market, Oil Minister Hardeep Singh Puri said on Oct. 16, reflecting the resilience of India's refining sector despite being the world's third largest crude consumer and importer.
"Who would have thought that in today's global turmoil, India would be exporting 1 million b/d refined products," Puri said at an event.
India's refining capacity is set to be 5.81 million b/d in the fiscal year 2026-27 (April-March), the minister said, reflecting an 8.6% increase from the current capacity of 5.35 million b/d due to brownfield expansions in four refineries by the end of the current fiscal year in March 2027.
India aims at 6.4 million b/d refining capacity by 2032 in order to cater to the growing domestic demand for refined oil products, according to the oil ministry, reflecting a 19.63%% increase from the current level of refining capacity in Asia's third-largest economy.
India's state refiners hold a 58% share in the country's total refining capacity of 5.35 million b/d, according to the oil ministry.
India has 24 refineries that include 18 state-owned, four units by private refiners and two refineries under a joint venture, according to the ministry's latest update.
India, the world's fourth biggest refiner after the US, China and Russia, meets around 88% of domestic demand via imports, the oil ministry said.
-- Ratnajyoti Dutta
US sanctions relief could free some oil tankers, but impact unclear: analysts
Shadow fleet ownership obscures return potential
Vessel age limits mainstream commercial appeal

16, down from a record high of $300/mt on Oct. 8, but up from $45.93/mt on Oct. 16, 2025.
Oil flows through the Middle East have rebounded, but the way in which transits through the Strait of Hormuz are conducted can intensify shipping bottlenecks and further increase freight.
The US had sanctioned 820 oil and LPG tankers as of the end of August, five more than a month earlier, according to an S&P Global Commodities at Sea report published on Sept. 30. The US, UK and EU lists together covered 1,226 unique vessels or roughly 11% of the global fleet.
Potential relief could range from removing individual ships from the US Specially Designated Nationals list to issuing timelimited or transaction-specific licenses permitting certain ships to carry approved cargoes. The market impact would depend heavily on whether Washington granted narrow waivers or restored ships' broader access to US-linked finance, insurance and maritime services, shipping analysts told Platts.
The impacts could also be limited by the age and suitability, as well as whether mainstream market participants were willing to take on the risks associated with previously sanctioned ships.
"The impact on Suezmaxes, Aframaxes, and LR2s will be de minimis," Jonathan Chappell, Evercore ISI senior managing director, said, citing the limited number of modern vessels in those categories. "VLCCs have the potential to be more meaningful, upward of 45-50, but they'd have to be accepted by traders, refiners, oil majors, have proper vettings, be in the right areas, and trade immediately."
"I think there could be a bit of the incredible upside taken out of the VLCC market, but not nearly enough to return rates to anything resembling pre-conflict levels," Chappell said.

Potential US sanctions relief for oil tankers could return some ships to mainstream trade, but overlapping European restrictions, opaque ownership and the advanced age of much of the shadow fleet may limit the amount of commercially usable capacity released into the global market, analysts said.
In a video to the World Petroleum Congress on Oct. 15, US Energy Secretary Chris Wright acknowledged the possibility of relaxing sanctions on shadow-fleet tankers linked to Russia, Venezuela and Iran in a US bid to combat soaring freight costs. The US Treasury has not offered further details and did not respond to a request for comment.
Global tanker rates have soared to record highs. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Very Large Crude carrier freight from the US Gulf Coast to China at $257.41/metric ton on Oct.

Participants said the impacts would depend on implementation and the overlapping sanctions regimes of nonUS countries.
"It still lacks operational clarity," a VLCC broker said Oct. 15. Ships remaining under UK or EU sanctions could still face restrictions even after receiving US relief. Of the sanctioned fleet at the end of August, 209 tankers appeared on all three jurisdictions' lists, while other ships were covered by two jurisdictions, according to the CAS report. Only a portion was

listed solely by the US. In the Aframax and Long Range 2 markets, sanctioned ships
accounted for 28% of global capacity, the highest proportion among tanker classes, CAS said.
"At the end of August, the sanctioned tanker fleet accounted for roughly 11% of the global fleet," the report said, adding that the fleet had grown 126% since the start of 2025.
Age, ownership hurdles However, delisting would not necessarily translate one-for-one
into additional mainstream tanker supply. Beneficial ownership was unidentified for 78% of sanctioned
ships in August, according to CAS. At least 14 sanctioned ships changed beneficial ownership during the month, while 48 changed registry, including 17 net additions to the Russian flag. Such ownership and flag histories could complicate acceptance by charterers, banks, classification societies and insurers even if US restrictions were removed.
The age of sanctioned ships could present another obstacle. CAS estimated that 51% of the broader shadow fleet was more than 20 years old, including 66% of its VLCCs and more than 80% of its LPG carriers. Older ships could remain commercially segregated or command discounts regardless of their formal sanctions status.
"Less than five ships are under 20 years that could return to the market," in the Aframax and LR2 segments, Chappell said.
CAS counted 2,246 shadow-fleet ships in August. Its definition includes ships that transported Iranian or Russian oil within the preceding two calendar years, US-sanctioned ships and those using unknown or suspected false flags, as well as some ships previously involved in sanctioned Venezuelan trade.
More than 1,600 ships, or 73% of the shadow fleet, had been linked to Russian trade during the previous two years, according to CAS. Shadow ships also represented 45% of the global Aframax/LR2 fleet.
Whether relief would materially pressure freight would hinge on how many ships could secure mainstream employment rather than the number formally delisted. The demand response could also offset some of the additional supply if relief enabled higher oil exports or shifted cargoes from ship-to-ship movements into direct voyages.
"While increased redirection amid Red Sea attacks and the blockade of the Iranian fleet contribute to the freight market squeeze, we think ship-to-ship transfers are the main driver of the freight rates rally," a Goldman Sachs Commodities Research report said on Oct. 12. "High freight rates push refiners to opt for closer-located crude to save on long-haul freight costs, widening regional differentials, especially in physical crude prices."
Sanctioned ships carried 66% of Russian seaborne crude exports in August, up from 58% in July, according to CAS data. Russia exported 4.088 million barrels/day of seaborne crude in September, up 8% month over month and up 3% year over year, despite ongoing sanctions.
-- Eamonn Brennan

NYMEX November RBOB settled up 5.52 cents/gallon at $3.2354/gal, and November ULSD ended the session 5.35 cents/ gal higher at $4.8991/gal.
Crude prices had been under pressure early in the session after the International Energy Agency Governing Board said late Oct. 15 that members will accelerate the release of emergency oil stocks, including more than 20 million barrels of diesel.
Some members have released more barrels than initially pledged, while others intend to do so, meaning the ultimate draw will exceed the original 400 million-barrel commitment, the agency said.
"The US probably won't be happy with the scale of the release, particularly regarding diesel. Initial suggestions were that we could see around 50 million barrels of diesel released. The risk is that if the US believes these volumes are not enough, the administration may mull restrictions on US diesel exports once again," ING's Warren Patterson said.
Black Sea war risk premiums surge amid attacks War risk premiums for insuring crude shipments from Black
Sea ports have reached their highest level in at least four years amid intensifying maritime attacks in the region.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the additional war risk premium at $4.60/barrel on Oct. 16, up from $4/b a day earlier, $3/b on Oct. 12, and the highest since the assessment began in October 2022.
Security consultancy Vanguard TECH reported that two tankers caught fire off Romania on Oct. 15, as the country's defense ministry reported drone attacks. Eight crew members aboard the ship Wine Trader went missing during the incident, according to Vanguard.
Since early summer, Russia and Ukraine have launched more intense attacks on merchant ships involved in each other's trades, with consultancy Windward estimating 86 ships attacked during July-September, up from 23 in the first half of 2026.

In the filing made with the Securities Exchange Commission, ExxonMobil estimated that margin changes would provide a $4.5 billion earnings uplift for Energy Products versus Q2, by far the largest positive factor affecting quarterly results.
The company said that stronger refining margins and favorable market conditions across fuels and trading activities more than offset several headwinds experienced earlier in the year.

Additional positive contributions are expected from lower maintenance impacts and seasonal timing benefits.
While ExxonMobil stressed that these disclosures are not formal earnings guidance, the company signaled that Energy Products should be a major contributor to improved third-quarter results as robust refining margins, fuels marketing performance and commodity-market volatility support earnings ahead of its Oct. 30 earnings release.
During its second quarter results call, Exxon entered Q3 expecting Energy Products earnings to strengthen as refining margins remain elevated amid global diesel shortages, constrained product supplies and strong refinery utilization across its integrated refining network.

REFINERY NEWS: Bilbao refinery halts desulfurization units
Refinery: Petroleos del Norte SA, Bilbao, Spain
Owner: 86% Repsol SA, 14% Kutxabank SA
Overall capacity: 11 million mt/y, or 220,000 b/d
The Bilbao refinery operator, Petroleos del Norte, SA, or Petronor, was halting the G3 gas oil desulfurization unit, while the N2 naphtha desulfurization unit would be shut for maintenance, it said in a statement dated Oct. 15.
It did not give a timeframe for either. The N2 unit was restarted twice in September after outages, according to previous company announcements, on Sept. 10 and Sept. 1. It tripped along with several other units on Aug. 31 due to an unplanned halt in two cogeneration units. These included the fluid catalytic cracker, the V3 vacuum unit in plant 3, the P2 platforming unit and the pressure swing absorption unit in the HD3 desulfurization unit in plant 2.

Subscriber Notes
Platts to amend freight calculation for Oman, Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, will amend the freight calculation used in its daily Oman and Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
Under the change, Platts will use the daily VLCC Gulf of Omanto-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (DOOEW00), which was launched Aug. 3, in its calculation of the outright price of Oman and Murban delivered into North Asia at 4:30 pm Singapore time (0830 GMT).
The change will better reflect the shipping economics for the two grades, with Murban primarily loading from Fujairah port and Oman from Mina Al Fahal port outside the Strait of Hormuz.
Currently, the freight calculation is based on the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (TDAAB00).
Platts first proposed the change in a subscriber note published Sept. 28, 2026.
The freight calculation will continue to use the conversion factor for metric tons to barrels, as applied to the grades, in line with their prevailing qualities. The conversion factors are currently 7.32 for Oman and 7.62 for Murban.
In line with this change, Platts will also use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East worldscale freight assessment (DIOEW00) in the freight calculation for Murban in its Indonesian Minas, Cinta and Widuri crude market parity prices.

Platts amends Abu Dhabi crude OSPs publication Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, has updated its publication
of Abu Dhabi crude official selling prices effective Oct. 8, 2026, in line with changes announced by Abu Dhabi National Oil Co. (ADNOC).
In July, ADNOC said it would adopt a prompt-month pricing methodology based on the Platts Dubai benchmark (PCAAT00) plus an ADNOC-announced differential for the OSPs of its Abu Dhabi onshore and offshore crude grades, with effect from November 2026 loadings. ADNOC added that it would announce the OSP differentials for Murban, Das, Umm Lulu and Upper Zakum in the month prior to the delivery month.
Accordingly, Platts will publish the monthly OSP differentials for the four grades, basis Platts Dubai, beginning with the November differentials issued by ADNOC on Oct. 8, 2026.
Platts first announced the change in a subscriber note published on Aug. 31, 2026.
The OSP differentials are published under the following

reinstated codes:
Grade Murban Umm Lulu Das Blend Upper Zakum

OSP Diff to Dubai AAIOS00 OSLUD00 AAXPL00 AAIOV00

Platts previously published the OSP differentials under these codes until May 2021, when they were then suspended due to a change in the ADNOC pricing methodology.
From November, the existing codes for the outright OSPs for Murban (AAIKZ00), Upper Zakum (AAILC00), Das Blend (AAXPH00) and Umm Lulu (OSLUF00) will remain unchanged. Platts will publish the outright OSPs of the four grades following the publication of the average of Platts Dubai crude assessments during the month of loading.
In view of the changes, Platts is no longer updating the codes reflecting Upper Zakum, Das Blend and Umm Lulu OSP differentials against Murban, as well as the codes for all four grades, including Murban, calculated against the monthly average of Platts cash Dubai 1st Line assessment in the month of trade.
The following codes will no longer be updated from October:

Grade Murban Umm Lulu Das Blend Upper Zakum

Diff to Dubai AOSPA00 AOSPB00 AOSPC00 AOSPD00

Diff to Murban OSP
AOSPE00 AOSPF00 AOSPG00

ADNOC's OSPs are published in the Platts price database and on Platts Global Alert page 1051.

Platts to launch daily FOB Middle East bitumen assessments from Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, will launch daily VG30 and VG40 bitumen spot assessments for FOB Middle East, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
The new assessments will complement Platts' existing daily and weekly bitumen assessments, providing greater price transparency and information in the waterborne trade for the product in the Asia-Pacific and Middle East markets.
Platts will launch two new daily outright assessments and their respective monthly averages. These assessments will reflect bulk viscosity grade 30 and VG40 grade bitumen, as per IS 73:2013 specifications, loading 10-30 days forward from the date of publication in the Middle East.

Pricing information for material with other specifications, such as PEN 60/70, may be considered for the assessments, normalized to the proposed standard.
The assessments will reflect market value at the close of Asian trading at 4:30 pm Singapore time and follow the Singapore publication schedule. Bids, offers and trades reported throughout the day may be considered in the assessment process, with values normalized to the market close at 4:30 pm Singapore time.

Platts proposes to amend rollover date of Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the rollover date of its Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close assessment to the first publication day of the month, from Dec. 1, 2026.
The proposed change, which follows Platts review of historical data sets, would align the ADB Strip at London close assessment with the current rollover date of its ADB Strip at Asia close assessment.
Currently, this assessment, which reflects the forward value of Dated Brent two months ahead of the date of publication at the 4:30 pm London close, rolls over on the 9th of the month.
The assessments affected by this change appear in Crude

Oil Marketwire, Asia Pacific/Arab Gulf Marketscan, Oilgram Price Report, Platts Global Alert/ Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 2206, 2213, 2215, 2217, 2219, 2260, 2261, 2262, 2263, 2264, 2265, 2266, 2267, 2268, 2269, 2270, 2271, 2280, 2281, 2558, 2559, and under their price database codes as below:

2-DAY TRAINING
Upstream Fundamentals and Economics
June 2-3 | Houston

Fossil fuels dominate the energy mix representing over 80% of global energy demand. With geopolitical tensions influencing supply volumes and lower price, the S&P Global Commodity Insights "Upstream Fundamentals and Economics" course will equip you with the insights and strategies needed to navigate a constantly changing environment, meet challenges with informed decisions, and drive business growth.
Our prestigious Upstream Fundamentals and Economics Training is a great opportunity to hear from a range of S&P Global experts sharing their expertise, regional trends and industry insights, as well as concrete case studies and analyses.
